       1.       INTRODUCTION

       1.1      GENERAL

       This report presents the results of our updated geotechnical study for the proposed single-family
       residential structure located at 14812 South Oak Bluff Place in Draper, Utah. The general
       location of the site is shown on Figure 1, Vicinity Map. The proposed development lot is
       0.48 acres in area, as shown on Figure 2, Area Map. The locations of our geological
       exploration trench, as well as current and previous test pits are also shown on Figure 2, Area
       Map. A previous geotechnical study for the property was conducted by our office in 20161.
       Additionally, a Geologic Hazard Evaluation and Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis was
       performed concurrently with this updated geotechnical study. The conclusions and
       recommendations of the previous geotechnical report and concurrent geologic hazard
       evaluation and geotechnical slope stability analysis have been incorporated into this report.

       During the course of this study, many of the conclusions and recommendations were presented
       to the design team and owner.

1.2    OBJECTIVES AND SCOPE

The objectives and scope of our study were planned in discussions between Mr. Adam Breen of
Breen Homes and Mr. Patrick Emery of Gordon Geotechnical Engineering, Inc. (G2).

In general, the objectives of this study were to:

       1.      Accurately define and evaluate the subsurface soil and groundwater conditions
               across the site.

       2.      Provide appropriate foundation, earthwork, subdrain, and geoseismic
               recommendations to be utilized in the design and construction of the proposed
               facility.

In accomplishing these objectives, our scope has included the following:

       1.      A field program consisting of the excavating, logging, and sampling of four test
               pits and one continuous trench.

       2.      A laboratory testing program.

       3.      An office program consisting of the correlation of available data, engineering
               analyses, and the preparation of this summary report.

1.3    AUTHORIZATION

Authorization was provided by returning a signed copy of our Professional Services Agreement
No. 19-1023 dated October 24, 2019 and executed October 30, 2019.

1.4    PROFESSIONAL STATEMENTS

Supporting data upon which our recommendations are based are presented in subsequent
sections of this report. Recommendations presented herein are governed by the physical
properties of the soils encountered in the exploration test pits and trenches, measured and
projected groundwater conditions, and the layout and design data discussed in Section 2.,
Proposed Construction, of this report. If subsurface conditions other than those described in
this report are encountered and/or if design and layout changes are implemented, G2 must be
informed so that our recommendations can be reviewed and amended, if necessary.

Our professional services have been performed, our findings developed, and our
recommendations prepared in accordance with generally accepted engineering principles and
practices in this area at this time.

2.     PROPOSED CONSTRUCTION

A single-family residential structure is planned for the 0.48-acre lot. The proposed structure is
anticipated to be two to three levels above grade with a full-depth basement level and of wood-
frame and concrete construction. Maximum column and wall loads are projected to be on the
order of 40 to 60 kips and 2 to 3 kips per lineal foot, respectively.

Site development will require a moderate amount of earthwork in the form of site grading. It is
estimated that maximum cuts and fills to achieve design grades will be on the order of four to
eight feet.

3.     INVESTIGATIONS

3.1    FIELD PROGRAM

In order to define and evaluate the subsurface soil and groundwater conditions across the site,
4 test pits were excavated to depths of 8 to 17 feet below existing grade. The approximate
locations of the test pits are presented on Figure 2. As part of our geologic hazard evaluation
analysis, a continuous exploration trench 84 feet in length was also excavated and logged to
evaluate landslide hazards for the site. The location of the trench is also presented on Figure 2.
The test pit locations excavated in conjunction with our previous 2016 geotechnical study are
also shown on Figure 2.

The field portion of our study was under the direct control and continual supervision of
experienced members of our geotechnical and engineering geology staff. During the course of
the excavation operations, a continuous log of the subsurface conditions encountered was
maintained. In addition, samples of the typical soils encountered were obtained for subsequent
laboratory testing and examination. The soils were classified in the field based upon visual and
textural examination. These classifications have been supplemented by subsequent inspection
and testing in our laboratory. Detailed graphical representation of the subsurface conditions
encountered is presented on Figures 4A through 4D, Log of Test Pits. Trench Logs are
included with the concurrent geologic hazard evaluation and slope stability analysis report.
Soils were classified in accordance with the nomenclature described on Figure 5, Unified Soil
Classification System.

Disturbed bag samples were collected from the soils brought up by the backhoe bucket.
Additionally, relatively undisturbed samples were obtained using 2.42-inch inside diameter hand
sampling equipment.

Following completion of excavating and logging, each test pit and continuous trench was
backfilled. Although an effort was made to compact the backfill with the backhoe, backfill was
not placed in uniform lifts and compacted to a specific density. Consequently, settlement of the
backfill with time is likely to occur.

4.      SITE CONDITIONS

4.1     SURFACE

The site consists of a roughly rectangular-shaped parcel of vacant land. The site is covered
with a light growth of grasses, weeds, and numerous scrub oak trees. The site is bordered by
undeveloped land to the north; existing residential structures border the site to the east, south,
and west.

The overall topography of the site slopes downhill to the northeast with an overall relief on the
order of 40 to 50 feet. The western portion of the site contains a densely vegetated slope at up
to 47 percent in steepness and 25 feet in height. The eastern portion of the site generally

                                                                                            Page 5
Breen Homes
                                                                                GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                        G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                            2

Updated Geotechnical Study                                                      ENGINEERING, INC.
August 14, 2020



consists of a flatter slope at less than 25 percent. The relatively sharp slope break running
roughly north-south through the approximate center of the site corresponds closely to a surficial
contact between weathered ash deposits and indurated block and ash bedrock.

Representative photographs of the site area are shown on Figure 6, Photographs.

4.2    SUBSURFACE SOIL AND BEDROCK

The soil conditions encountered in each of the test pits and trench, to the depths excavated,
were relatively similar. From the surface and extending to a depth of seven feet in Test Pit TP-3
are silty sand and gravel fills. The fills will exhibit variable and, in most cases, poor engineering
characteristics.

From the surface and extending to a depth of 4 to 17+ feet in Test Pits TP-1, TP-1A, TP-2,
TP-2A, TP-3A, TP-4A, and underlying the fills in Test Pit TP-3, is highly plastic clays (fat clays).
The fat clay soils are brown to reddish-brown in color, moist, very stiff, and contain some fine
sand and gravel. The fat clays exhibit moisture sensitive characteristics; that is, soils will
expand with significant increase in moisture content. The fat clay soil corresponds to the
weathered ash deposits identified in the geologic hazard evaluation report.

Underlying the fat clays at the test pit locations, weathered bedrock consisting of indurated
block and ash volcanic rocks was encountered. The weathered bedrock is white to light 5gray to
light reddish-brown in color, soft grading to moderately hard with depth, and dry to slightly moist.
The indurated block and ash bedrock will exhibit high strength, low compressibility, and non-
moisture sensitive characteristics under the anticipated loading range.

The lines designating the interface between soil types on the test pit logs generally represent
approximate boundaries. In-situ, the transition between soil types may be gradual.

4.3    GROUNDWATER

Groundwater was not encountered in the test pits or continuous trench during excavation
operations. Groundwater is projected to be at depths of approximately 50 to 100 feet below the
east and west portions of the site, respectively. Although the projected static water table is
deep, it has been our experience in the area that “perched” groundwater conditions may
develop during late spring and early summer months.

5.     DISCUSSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

5.1    SUMMARY OF FINDINGS

The site is suitable for the proposed development from a geotechnical standpoint provided that
the recommendations provided herein are incorporated into the design and construction of the
proposed structure. The most significant geotechnical aspects of the site are:

       1.     Stability of the natural slope.

The results of our concurrent geologic hazard evaluation and geotechnical slope stability
analysis (separate report) indicate that the weathered ash deposits (fat clay) on the
northeastern portion of the site have been deformed due to past block failure movement.
According to our analysis, the factor of safety against slope failure of the weathered ash
deposits is less than the Draper City required minimum of 1.5. Additionally, the slope is at risk
of failure during the design seismic event.

The most preferable solution, from a geotechnical/geologic engineering standpoint, is hazard
avoidance. If other project constraints will allow, the proposed structure may be located off of
the unstable slide mass (fat clay). The indurated block and ash bedrock on the hillside above
the weathered ash deposits (uphill of the shear zone identified in the exploration trench for the
geologic hazards study) is stable and is suitable from a slope stability hazard standpoint.

In summary, the steeper slopes in the southeast portion of the site, formed by the indurated
block and ash bedrock, meets the required factor of safety against slope instability. The lower
portion of the site (where the fat clay was encountered) does not meet the required factor of
safety against slope stability.

Based on the observed angle of the shear zone, and depth to bedrock encountered in the
exploration trench and test pits, we have generated a “Recommended Buildable Area”, shown
on Figure 3, Recommended Buildable Area. With this area shown, stabile indurated block and
ash bedrock is projected to be encountered within 15 feet of the existing ground surface.
Structures located within the “Recommended Buildable Area” may be supported on
conventional spread and wall foundations extending to block and ash bedrock and/or structural
fill extending to block and ash bedrock. Structures within the “Recommended Buildable Zone”
will meet the required factor of safety against slope instability. Structures established outside
the “Recommended Buildable Area” would need to be supported on deep foundations designed
to resist very high shear forces that would be generated during a slope failure. Based on our
experience, the shear resistant deep foundations required would be uneconomical for
residential construction.

       2.     The expansive characteristics of the fat clays encountered at the site.



                                                                                           Page 7
Breen Homes
                                                                                  GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                          G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                              2

Updated Geotechnical Study                                                        ENGINEERING, INC.
August 14, 2020



Due to the slope stability concerns and expansive characteristics of the fat clay encountered at
the site, fat clays must be completely removed from below foundations. The proposed structure
must be established entirely on stable indurated block and ash bedrock or structural fill
extending to indurated block and ash bedrock.

Fat clays may potentially remain below floor slabs provided the floor slab is established on a
minimum of two feet of granular structural fill. It is our opinion that with proper control of the
moisture content of the supporting soils beneath at-grade slabs, vertical movements can be
appropriately minimized. See Section 5.6, Floor Slabs, and Section 5.10, Water Control, for
more details.

        3.     The potential for “perched” groundwater conditions.

Due to the potential for “perched” groundwater conditions to develop at the site, a foundation
subdrain system is recommended.

Detailed discussions pertaining to slope stability, earthwork, foundations, floor slabs, lateral
resistance, subdrains, and the geoseismic setting of the site are discussed in the following
sections.

5.2     EARTHWORK

5.2.1   Site Preparation

Preparation of the site must consist of the removal of all non-engineered fills (if encountered),
vegetation, loose surficial soils, fat clay soil, topsoil, debris, and other deleterious materials from
beneath an area extending at least three feet beyond the perimeter of the proposed building,
rigid pavement, and exterior flatwork areas.

Subsequent to the above operations and prior to the placement of footings, structural site
grading fill or floor slabs, the exposed natural subgrade must be proofrolled by passing
moderate-weight rubber tire-mounted construction equipment over the surface at least twice. If
any loose, soft, or disturbed zones are encountered, they must be completely removed in
footing and floor slab areas and replaced with granular structural fill. If removal depth required
is greater than two feet, G2 must be notified to provide further recommendations. In pavement
areas, unsuitable soils encountered during recompaction and proofrolling must be removed to a
maximum depth of two feet and replaced with compacted granular structural fill.

5.2.2   Excavations

Temporary construction excavations not exceeding four feet in depth may be constructed with
near-vertical sideslopes. Excavations up to 10 feet in fine-grained cohesive soils can be
constructed with sideslopes no steeper than three-quarters horizontal to one vertical


                                                                                                 Page 8
Breen Homes
                                                                                   GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                           G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                               2

Updated Geotechnical Study                                                         ENGINEERING, INC.
August 14, 2020



(0.75H:1.0V). Excavations up to 10 feet in the weathered bedrock may be constructed with
sideslopes no steeper than one-half horizontal to one vertical (0.5H:1.0V). If saturated granular
soils are encountered (not anticipated at the site), flatter sideslopes, shoring and bracing, and/or
dewatering will be required.

Test pit and trench excavations at the site and past experience in the area indicates excavations
of 5 to 10 feet into bedrock can generally be accomplished by using heavy construction
equipment and possibly “rippers”.

All excavations must be inspected periodically by qualified personnel. If any signs of instability
are noted, immediate remedial action must be initiated.

5.2.3   Structural Fill

Structural fill is defined as all fill which will ultimately be subjected to structural loadings, such as
imposed by footings, floor slabs, pavements, etc. Structural fill will be required as backfill over
foundations and utilities, as site grading fill, and in some areas, as replacement fill below
footings. All structural fill must be free of sod, rubbish, topsoil, frozen soil, and other deleterious
materials. Structural site grading fill is defined as fill placed over fairly large open areas to raise
the overall site grade. For structural site grading fill, the maximum particle size should generally
not exceed four inches; although, occasional larger particles, not exceeding six inches in
diameter may be incorporated if placed randomly in a manner such that “honeycombing” does
not occur and the desired degree of compaction can be achieved. The maximum particle size
within structural fill placed within confined areas should generally be restricted to two inches.

The on-site fat clay soils are not recommended for use as structural site grading fill. Indurated
block and ash bedrock, when broken up, may potentially be utilized as structural site grading fill
provided any oversize material is removed.

To stabilize soft subgrade conditions or where structural fill is required to be placed below a
level one foot above the water table at the time of construction, a mixture of coarse gravels and
cobbles and/or one and one-half- to two-inch gravel (stabilizing fill) should be utilized.

Non-structural site grading fill is defined as all fill material not designated as structural fill and
may consist of any cohesive or granular soils not containing excessive amounts of degradable
material.

5.2.4   Fill Placement and Compaction

Structural fill shall be placed in lifts not exceeding eight inches in loose thickness. Structural fills
shall be compacted in accordance with the percent of the maximum dry density as determined
by the AASHTO2 T-180 (ASTM3 D-1557) compaction criteria in accordance with the following
table:

                                                          Total Fill
                                                         Thickness      Minimum Percentage of
                       Location                            (feet)        Maximum Dry Density
        Beneath an area extending at least 3 feet
          beyond the perimeter of the structure             0 to 8                    95

              Outside area defined above                    0 to 6                    90

              Outside area defined above                    6 to 8                    92

                        Road base                              -                      96


Structural fills greater than eight feet thick are not anticipated at the site.

Subsequent to stripping and prior to the placement of structural site grading fill, the subgrade
must be prepared as discussed in Section 5.2.1, Site Preparation, of this report. In confined
areas, subgrade preparation should consist of the removal of all loose or disturbed soils.

Coarse gravel and cobble mixtures (stabilizing fill), if utilized, shall be end-dumped, spread to a
maximum loose lift thickness of 15 inches, and compacted by dropping a backhoe bucket onto
the surface continuously at least twice. As an alternative, the fill may be compacted by passing
moderately heavy construction equipment or large self-propelled compaction equipment at least
twice. Subsequent fill material placed over the coarse gravels and cobbles shall be adequately
placed so that the “fines” are “worked into” the voids in the underlying coarser gravels and
cobbles.

Non-structural fill may be placed in lifts not exceeding 12 inches in loose thickness and
compacted by passing construction, spreading, or hauling equipment over the surface at least
twice.

5.2.5   Utility Trenches

All utility trench backfill material below structurally loaded facilities (flatwork, floor slabs, roads,
etc.) should be placed at the same density requirements established for structural fill. If the
surface of the backfill becomes disturbed during the course of construction, the backfill should
be proofrolled and/or properly compacted prior to the construction of any exterior flatwork over a
backfilled trench. Proofrolling may be performed by passing moderately loaded rubber tire-
mounted construction equipment uniformly over the surface at least twice. If excessively loose
or soft areas are encountered during proofrolling, they should be removed to a maximum depth
of two feet below design finish grade and replaced with structural fill.

Most utility companies and City-County governments are now requiring that Type A-1 or A-1-a
(AASHTO Designation – basically granular soils with limited fines) soils be used as backfill over
utilities. These organizations are also requiring that in public roadways the backfill over major
utilities be compacted over the full depth of fill to at least 96 percent of the maximum dry density
as determined by the AASHTO T-180 (ASTM D-1557) method of compaction. We recommend
that as the major utilities continue onto the site that these compaction specifications are
followed.

The on-site fat clay soils are not recommended for use as trench backfill. Indurated block and
ash bedrock, when broken up, may potentially be utilized as utility trench backfill.

5.3     SPREAD AND CONTINUOUS WALL FOUNDATIONS

5.3.1   Design Data

The proposed structure may be supported upon conventional spread and continuous wall
foundations established upon suitable indurated block and ash bedrock or structural fill
extending to indurated block and ash bedrock. Under no circumstances shall footings be placed
overlying non-engineered fills or fat clay soils.

For design, the following parameters are provided with respect to the projected loading
discussed in Section 2., Proposed Construction, of this report:

        Minimum Recommended Depth of Embedment for
          Frost Protection                                                     - 42 inches

          Non-frost Conditions                                                 - 15 inches

            For footings on indurated block and ash bedrock and/or
            structural fill extending to indurated block and ash bedrock       - 3,500 pounds
                                                                                 per square foot

        Bearing Pressure Increase
          for Seismic Loading                                                  - 50 percent*

        *   Not applicable for edge bearing pressure when the footings are established upon
            granular soil. Use 25 percent for overturning or other inclined loading.

The term “net bearing pressure” refers to the pressure imposed by the portion of the structure
located above lowest adjacent final grade. Therefore, the weight of the footing and backfill to
the lowest adjacent final grade need not be considered. Real loads are defined as the total of
all dead plus frequently applied live loads. Total load includes all dead and live loads, including
seismic and wind.

5.3.2   Installation

Under no circumstances shall the footings be established upon non-engineered fills, expansive
soils, loose or disturbed soils, rubbish, construction debris, other deleterious materials, frozen
soils, or within ponded water. If unsuitable soils are encountered, they must be completely
removed and replaced with compacted structural fill.

The width of structural replacement fill below footings should be equal to the width of the footing
plus one foot for each foot of fill thickness.

5.3.3   Settlements

Settlements of foundations designed and installed in accordance with above recommendations
and supporting maximum projected structural loads are anticipated to be on the order of one inch
or less. Settlements are expected to occur rapidly with approximately 60 to 70 percent of the
settlements occurring during construction.

5.4     LATERAL RESISTANCE

Lateral loads imposed upon foundations due to wind or seismic forces may be resisted by the
development of passive earth pressures and friction between the base of the footings and the
supporting soils. In determining frictional resistance on indurated block and ash bedrock, a
coefficient of 0.45 should be utilized. Passive resistance provided by properly placed and
compacted granular structural fill above the water table may be considered equivalent to a fluid
with a density of 300 pounds per cubic foot. Below the water table, this granular soil should be
considered equivalent to a fluid with a density of 150 pounds per cubic foot.

A combination of passive earth resistance and friction may be utilized provided that the friction
component of the total is divided by 1.5.

5.5     LATERAL PRESSURES

The lateral pressure parameters, as presented within this section, assume that the backfill
extending at least five feet from the back of the wall be properly placed and compacted granular
soil. Indurated block and ash bedrock, if meeting the requirement of granular soil, may be
utilized as backfill behind retaining walls and around below grade facilities. Fat clay is not
recommended as backfill. If indurated block and ash bedrock cannot be used or does not meet
the requirement of granular soil, imported granular soil may be required. The lateral pressures
imposed upon subgrade facilities will, therefore, be basically dependent upon the relative rigidity
and movement of the backfilled structure. For active walls, such as retaining walls which can
move outward (away from the backfill), granular backfill may be considered equivalent to a fluid
with a density of 35 pounds per cubic foot in computing lateral pressures. For more rigid
basement walls, granular backfill may be considered equivalent to a fluid with a density of 45
pounds per cubic foot. For very rigid non-yielding walls, granular backfill should be considered
equivalent to a fluid with a density of at least 55 pounds per cubic foot. The above values
assume that the surface of the soils slope behind the wall is horizontal, that the granular fill has
been placed and lightly compacted, not as structural fill. If the fill is placed as a structural fill the
values should be increased to 45 pounds per cubic foot, 60 pounds per cubic foot, and 120
pounds per cubic foot, respectively.

Recommended average lateral uniform pressure for various height walls are tabulated on the
following page and assume a granular wall backfill with a horizontal grade above the wall. It
should be noted that the lateral pressures as quoted assume that the backfill materials will not

become saturated. If the backfill becomes saturated, the above values may be decreased by
one-half; however, full hydrostatic water pressures will have to be included.

                   Wall Height              Uniform Seismic Lateral Pressure*, **
                     (feet)                                (psf)
                         4                                     96
                         8                                    193
                        12                                    289
           * Maximum short-term pressures, they are not sustained loads.
          ** For intermediate height wall, the lateral pressure will be developed
             based upon linear interpolation between the pressures at the specific
             height.


Note that the pressures presented in this section do not include surcharge loadings, such as
floor slabs, adjacent footings, etc.

5.6    FLOOR SLABS

Because of the potentially expansive nature of the natural soils, it is our recommendation that
the slabs be underlain by at least two feet of well-graded granular fill containing at least
20 percent low plasticity fines. Settlements of lightly loaded floor slabs will be negligible.

Outside flatwork will be subject to frost and vertical movements due to the expansion of the
clays. To reduce this potential, all critical outside flatwork should be underlain by at least
18 inches of the granular structural fill, as specified previously.

5.7    SUBDRAINS

Due to the potential for “perched” groundwater conditions, a permanent foundation/chimney
subdrain system is recommended around the outside of all below grade walls.

The perimeter subdrain pipe should consist of a minimum of four-inch diameter, slotted or
perforated pipe with the invert established at least 18 inches below the top of the lowest
adjacent slab. The pipe should be encased in a one-half- to one-inch minus clean gap-graded
crushed gravel extending two inches below, laterally, and up continuously at least 12 inches
above the top of the lowest adjacent slab. The same granular material could be utilized as the
chimney drain against the subgrade walls. The gravel chimney drain must be continuous and at
least six inches wide. In all cases, the gravels must be separated from natural soils or backfill
with a geotextile fabric, such as Mirafi 140N or equivalent. As an alternate, a synthetic drain
board, such as Miradrain or equivalent, can be used for the chimney subdrain. The slope of the


                                                                                          Page 14
Breen Homes
                                                                              GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                      G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                          2

Updated Geotechnical Study                                                    ENGINEERING, INC.
August 14, 2020



pipe should be at least 0.25 percent to a suitable point of gravity discharge, such as a sump
within or outside the perimeter of the below-grade portion of the structure or by gravity down-
gradient. Prior to installing the gravels, we recommend that the outside walls adjacent to
habitable areas be appropriately waterproofed.        If the areas are mechanical areas,
dampproofing should be adequate.

5.8     GEOSEISMIC SETTING

5.8.1   General

As of July 2019, the State of Utah has adopted the International Building Code (IBC) 2018 and
is still using the International Residential Code (IRC) 2015. The IBC 2018 code determines the
seismic response for a site based upon 2014 mapping of bedrock accelerations prepared by the
United States Geologic Survey (USGS) and the soil site class. The USGS values are presented
on maps incorporated into the IBC code and are also available based on latitude and longitude
coordinates (grid points).

5.8.2   Faulting

Based on our review of available literature, no active faults pass through or are immediately
adjacent to the site.

5.8.3   Soil Class

For dynamic structural analysis, the Site Class C – Very Dense Soil and Soft Rock Profile as
defined in Table 20.3-1, Site Classification, of ASCE 7-16 can be utilized.

5.8.4   Ground Motions

The IBC 2018 code is based on 2014 USGS mapping, which provides peak values of short and
long period accelerations (SS, S1) for the Site Class B-C boundary for the Maximum Considered
Earthquake (MCE). This Site Class B-C boundary represents a hypothetical bedrock surface
and must be corrected for local soil conditions based on the site soil class. The following table
summarizes the peak ground and short and long period accelerations for this site for a MCE
event and incorporates a soil amplification factor for a Site Class C soil profile in the second
column.       Based on the site latitude and longitude (40.4818 degrees north
and -111.8403 degrees west, respectively), the values for this site are tabulated on the following
page.

Breen Homes

                                                    Boundary                  [adjusted for site
         Spectral Acceleration Value, T          [mapped values]                class effects]
                   Seconds                             (% g)                         (% g)

              Peak Ground Acceleration                  67.4                        80.9
  0.2 Seconds, (Short Period Acceleration)           SS = 147.2                  SMS = 176.6

      1.0 Seconds (Long Period Acceleration)          S1 = 55.1                  SM1 = 79.9


The IBC design accelerations (SDS and SD1) are based on multiplying the above accelerations
(SMS and SM1) for the MCE event by two-thirds (⅔).

The SDS of 117.7 corresponds to the IRC seismic design category D2.

5.8.5    Liquefaction

Liquefaction is defined as the condition when saturated, loose, finer-grained sand-type soils lose
their support capabilities because of excessive pore water pressure which develops during a
seismic event.

Due to the shallow bedrock and cohesive soils encountered at the site liquefaction is not
anticipated to occur during the design seismic event.

5.9      CEMENT TYPES

The laboratory tests indicate that the site soils contain negligible amounts of water-soluble
sulfates. Therefore, all concrete which will be in contact with the site soils may be prepared
using standard Type I cement.

5.10     WATER CONTROL

To reduce the possibility of water infiltration into potentially expansive soils, as a minimum the
following are recommended:

         1.       There should be a minimum 2 percent downward slope maintained away from
                  the building for a distance of at least 15 feet.

         2.       Water discharged from downspouts must be collected and discharged downslope
                  at least 15 feet from the proposed structure.

          3.     All water-conveying utilities should be checked for leakage prior to backfilling and
                 installed with flexible connections and joints.

          4.     Sprinkler heads should not be installed around the immediate perimeter of the
                 structure .

  5.11    SITE OBSERVATIONS

  A qualified geotechnical engineer from our staff must aid in verifying that suitable block and ash
  bedrock has been encountered prior to the placement of structural site grading fills, footings, or
  foundations.

       Re:      Revision No. 1
                Response to Review
                Geologic Hazard Evaluation – Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis
                Proposed Residential Structure
                Lot 27, Oak Vista No. 1 at Suncrest
                14812 South Oak Bluff Place
                Draper, Utah


       This letter presents Gordon Geotechnical Engineering, Inc. (G2) response to the Draper City
       reviews of our geological hazards, geotechnical and slope stability study for the above
       referenced property dated June 9, 2020. The geological review for this submitted study was
       provided by Simon Associates (SA) dated July 21, 2020, on behalf of Draper City (Draper City
       Case No: SUBD-000980-2020). Following are the SA review comments and our responses. A
       reissued Geologic Hazard Evaluation – Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis report, that
       includes revisions and edits in response to the SA comments will be prepared to accompany
       this review.

       SA Comment 1

       1. Section 9-19-100 G of the Draper City Geologic Hazards Ordinance stipulates the minimum
          items all geologic hazards reports must include. Item (2) in Section 9-19-100 G of the Draper
          City Geologic Hazards Ordinance mandates all geologic hazards reports shall include, at a
          minimum:

                “A detailed site map of the subject area, at a scale equal to or more detailed than one
                inch equals 200 feet (1” = 200’), showing the locations of subsurface investigations and
                site-specific geologic mapping performed as part of the geologic investigation, including
                boundaries and features related to any geologic hazards, topography, and drainage.

Gordon Geotechnical Engineering, Inc.                                                           Tel: 801-327-9600
4426 South Century Drive, Suite 100                                                             Fax: 801-327-9601
Salt Lake City, Utah 84123                                                                 www.gordongeotech.com

        The site map must show the location and boundaries of the property, geologic hazards,
        delineation of any recommended setback distances from hazards, and recommended
        locations for structures. Buildable and non-buildable areas shall be clearly identified;”
        note this map is generally referred to as a site geologic map or site-specific geologic
        map.

During the November 25, 2019, Scoping Meeting, Draper City requested all bedding, jointing,
and other geologic structures are shown on the site plan and/or geologic map, (see Item 12,
SA, 2019a). Presumably Figure 5, Site Details, of the June 9, 2020, G2 report, is intended to
fulfill Section 9-19-100 G(2) of the Draper City Geologic Hazards Ordinance. If SA’s
presumption is correct, SA recommends Draper City require G2 to depict the following on
Figure 5 of the June 9, 2020, G2 report:

   a.   Boundaries and features related to any geologic hazards, topography, and drainage;
   b.   Recommended setback distances from hazards;
   c.   Recommended locations for structures;
   d.   Buildable and non-buildable areas, and;
   e.   Geologic units documented during the investigative, including surficial units such as fill
        and colluvium.
   f.   Bedding, jointing, and other geologic structures.

G2 Response/Action Comment 1.a.

Geologic contact Tva – Tvb, drawn as block failure head scarp on Figure 5. The Boundaries of
the critical failure surface have been drawn on Line A-A’ on Figure 5.

G2 Response/Action Comment 1.b.

Mappable hazard features named in the Draper City Geologic Hazards Ordinance including
surface fault rupture; liquefaction hazards; alluvial fan processes including debris-flow; rockfall
and avalanche hazards, and flood hazards, are not present on the site vicinity, thus setbacks for
these hazards are not shown on Figure 5.

G2 Response/Action Comment 1.c.

The location for the structure shown on Figure 5, has been re-drawn based on Gilson
Engineering (2020) placement.

G2 Response/Action Comment 1.d.

“Recommended Building Area” envelope added to Figure 5, has been drawn showing areas of
the property where the bedrock is projected within 15 feet of surface, and proposed structure

SA Comment 2

2. On page 7 of the June 9, 2020, G2 report (Section 3.3, Field Program), G2 states, “To
   document the observed exposures in the trench, a geologic log of the subsurface conditions
   encountered was drawn. Detailed graphical representations of the subsurface conditions
   encountered are presented in Figure 6, Log of Trench…” The following items should be
   addressed in Figure 6.

   a. Station 0+00 corresponds to the west end of the trench. Figure 6 depicts Station 0+00 as
      the east end of the trench. SA recommends Draper City request G2 address the
      apparent discrepancy.
   b. SA recommends Draper City request G2 depict the orientation of the trench.
   c. SA recommends Draper City request G2 include an explanation/description for
      “Deformation Zone.”
   d. At about Station 0+60, the contact between Units 3 and 2A/2B is undefined. SA
      recommends Draper City request G2 address the apparent discrepancy.
   e. Between about Stations 0+41 and 0+59 the contact between Units 2A and 2B is a
      dashed line. SA recommends Draper City request G2 explain the dashed line.
   f. The baseline (0 feet) of the vertical scale on the part of the log from Stations 0+00 to
      0+50 does not match the baseline (0 feet) of the vertical scale on the part of the log from
      Stations 0+50 to 0+85.

SA recommends Draper City request G2:

   i.   Address the apparent discrepancy.

SA Comment 3

3.   On page 10 of the June 9, 2020, G2 report (Section 4.3 Subsurface Soil and Groundwater),
     G2 states, “Along the Unit 1 and Unit 2A boundary, an approximately four-inch zone of
     deformed Unit 1 material, coated with a thin FeOX (iron oxide) vein was observed. This
     boundary was interpreted during field review as a possible slope failure boundary, with
     block failure movement occurring between Unit 1 and Units 2A and 2B. The 14- to 18-
     degree dips observed in Units 2A and 2B are believed to be a result of back-tilting, as a
     result of this possible block failure movement.”

On pages 13 and 14 of the June 9, 2020, G2 report (Section 5.5, Landsliding and
Slope Stability), G2 states,

        “The soil and rock encountered in our trench shown in Figure 6, and described in
         Section 3.3, was interpreted as a possible slope failure boundary, with block failure
         movement occurring between Unit 1 and Units 2A and 2B.”
        “… we presume the possible slope failure boundary observed in the trench is the result
         of block failure movement.”

   a. G2’s use of "possible," when interpreting geologic features denotes there are at least
      two possible explanations for the particular geologic feature. SA recommends Draper
      City request G2 discuss the alternate explanation for the geologic slope failure boundary
      discussed on pages 10 and 13 of the June 9, 2020, G2 report.

   b. G2’s use of "possible" and” presume,” when interpreting geologic features denotes a
      degree of uncertainty, perhaps conclusions based on conjecture, rather than conclusions
      based on adequate data (see Section 9-19-100 G(6) in the Draper City Geologic
      Hazards Ordinance. SA recommends Draper City require all G2 conclusions:

       i    Be supported by adequate data, including additional field investigation data if
       deemed appropriate,

       ii.     Be clearly presented in the Consultant report.

G2 Response/Action Comment 3

The adjective “possible” will be removed from our discussion of the block failure interpretation in
our text; however, the term “presume” will be remain because our conclusions are based upon
data and observations and not assumptions.

   states, “Because our review of the historic [sic. historical] (predevelopment 1958 and 1962)
   aerial photography and LiDAR analysis (2014) did not reveal diagnostic arcuate rotational
   morphology indicative of typical rotation slumping (Varnes, 1978), we presume the possible
   slope failure boundary observed in the trench is the result of block failure movement.” SA

   b. A 1958 and a1962 photograph depicting the subject property.

        1.       INTRODUCTION

        1.1      GENERAL

        Gordon Geotechnical Engineering, Inc. (G2) has prepared this report for our Geologic Hazard
        Evaluation and Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis for the proposed single-family home
        construction referenced above. The site is located at 14812 South Oak Bluff Place in Draper,
        Utah, which is located as shown on Figure 1, Vicinity Map. The 0.48-acre development lot
        proposed for the construction is shown on Figure 2, Aerial Coverage. Oak Bluff Place (street)
        affronts the site on the east, with similar single-family development lots located on the north and
        south sides of the site, and open space land to the northwest of the site. The location of the
        proposed single-family home structure and our proposed geological exploration trench for this
        study is also shown on Figure 5, Site Details. It should be noted that the proposed structure
        location may change due to the findings of this study. A previous geotechnical study for the
        property was conducted by our office in 2016 (G2, 2016).

        1.2      DRAPER CITY GEOLOGIC HAZARDS REGULATIONS

        The subject property is located on a moderate to steep sloping hillside area associated with the
        Traverse Maintain Salient, and is within the Draper City Slope Stability Special Study Area,

which as 0a part of the Draper City Geological Hazards Ordinance, requires a
geotechnical/geological study be performed prior to site improvements. The Draper City
Geologic Hazards Ordinance Chapter 9-19-100 Geologic Hazards Reports, specifies that
“…each geologic hazards report shall be site-specific and shall identify all known or suspected
potential geologic hazards, originating on-site or off-site, whether previously identified or
previously unrecognized, that may affect the subject property…” Because the subject site is
within the slope stability investigation zones identified by Draper City, on the 9-19-161-1;
Appendix A, Geologic Hazards Study Area Maps, Plate A-3, Slope Stability Special Study Area
Map, a slope stability analysis and reporting as outlined in 9-19-161-3; Appendix C of the
Ordinance Chapter, Minimum Standards for Slope Stability Analysis shall be performed for the
site evaluation (Draper City, 2020).

Although landsliding has not been mapped on the site (Biek, 2005), we intend to evaluate the
site for its proposed use, and to analyze the existing slope conditions as they pertain to long-
term slope stability of the site.

1.3    OBJECTIVES AND SCOPE

The objectives and scope of our study were planned in discussions between Mr. Adam Breen of
Breen Homes and Mr. Patrick Emery of G2.

In general, the objectives of this study were to:

       1.      Perform a site-specific Geologic Hazards Analysis and Geotechnical Slope
               Stability Analysis compliant with Draper City Geologic Hazards Ordinance
               Chapter 9-19-100.

In accomplishing these objectives, our scope has included the following:

       1.      Work plan development and methodology for Geologic Hazards / Slope Stability
               site evaluation.

       2.      Schedule Scoping Meeting with Draper City staff, Draper City Engineering, and
               Geology consultants to present and review proposed work plan and secure
               Draper City excavation permit.

       3.      Field program including construction of a 90-foot long by 10-foot deep trench,
               and coordination of field review with Draper City Engineering and Geology
               consultants.

       4.      Geological and Geotechnical analysis including the development of site-specific
               geological cross-sections, and geotechnical slope stability analysis.

1.6    PROFESSIONAL STATEMENTS

Supporting data upon which our recommendations are based are presented in subsequent
sections of this report. Recommendations presented herein are governed by the physical
properties of the geological units and soils encountered in the exploration trench, projected
groundwater conditions, and the layout and design data discussed in Section 2., Proposed
Construction, of this report. If subsurface conditions other than those described in this report
are encountered and/or if design and layout changes are implemented, G2 must be informed so
that our recommendations can be reviewed and amended, if necessary.

Our professional services have been performed, our findings developed, and our
recommendations prepared in accordance with generally accepted engineering and geological
principles and practices in this area at this time.

2.     PROPOSED CONSTRUCTION

A single-family residential structure is proposed for the site. The proposed structure is
anticipated to be two to three levels above grade with a full-depth basement level and of wood-
frame and concrete construction. Projected maximum column and wall loads are on the order
of 40 to 60 kips and 2 to 3 kips per lineal foot, respectively. Site development will require a
moderate amount of earthwork in the form of site grading. We estimate that maximum cuts and
fills to achieve design grades will be on the order of two to eight feet.

3.     INVESTIGATIONS

3.1    PREVIOUS INVESTIGATIONS

A previous Geotechnical Study was conducted for the site by G2 in September 2016. The
previous study was conducted to evaluate the subsurface soil and groundwater conditions
across the site, and provide appropriate foundation, earthwork, pavement, and geoseismic

Breen Homes

recommendations to be utilized in the design and construction for the proposed residence. This
current report addresses the geologic hazards and slope stability for the site.

3.2    LITERATURE REVIEW AND SITE ANALYSIS

Our review of existing information and mapping for the subject site has included aforementioned
previous reports and documentation and previous Utah Geological Survey (UGS) maps, reports
and data (Biek, 2005; Christenson and Shaw, 2008). The geologic mapping of the site vicinity
by Biek (2005) is presented on Figure 3, Geologic Mapping. In addition to the previewed
reports, maps, and literature, our review has included an analysis of vertical-orthorectified and
stereoscopic aerial photography for the site including a 1958 1:10,000 stereoscopic sequence
(frames AAL-35V-94 and AAL-35V-94), a 1962 1:27,000 stereoscopic sequence (frames
1VAFZ00010061 and 1VAFZ00010062), a 2012 5.0-inch digital color HRO coverage (as shown
on Figure 2, Aerial Coverage, Figure 5, Site Details), and 2014 and 2018 0.6-meter digital color
NAIP coverage of the site.

A GIS analysis and data integration for the site evaluation was conducted using the QGIS® GIS
platform to geoprocess and analyze 2014 0.5-meter LiDAR digital elevation data made available
for the site by the Utah Automated Geographic Reference Center (AGRC), and presented on
Figure 4, LiDAR Analysis. The GIS analysis includes using the QGIS® platform Geospatial Data
Abstraction Library (GDAL) Contour, and the GRASS® (Geographic Resources Analysis
Support System) r.slope and r.shaded.relief modules. The GIS platform was used to integrate
terrain layers, geologic mapping, photogrametric details, available digital vector and raster data,
and field GPS details collected during our field program, and for graphical presentation of our
study findings herein.

3.3    FIELD PROGRAM

In order to define and evaluate the subsurface soil and groundwater conditions for the present
scope of work, an exploration trench was excavated and logged at the site on
December 10, 2019. The trench was 84.0 feet in length and extended to depths of 6.0 to
14.5 feet below the surface. The trench was excavated beneath the proposed structure location
using a track-mounted 30-ton class excavator at the location shown on Figure 5, Site Details.

Our field program was conducted under the supervision of Dr. Greg Schlenker, P.G. of our
engineering geology staff, and Mr. Patrick Emery, P.E. of our geotechnical staff was also
present and collected soil samples from the trench for laboratory testing.

On the behalf of Draper City, Mr. David Simon, P.G. (Simon Associates LLC) and
Mr. Alan Taylor, P.E. (Taylor Geotechnical), visited the site during the field program for field
review purposes, and observed the soil and rock exposed in the trench. During the
December 10, 2019 field review, informal discussions were held between the professionals



                                                                                             Page 6
Breen Homes
                                                                                  GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                          G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                              2
Geologic Hazard Evaluation – Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis
                                                                                  ENGINEERING, INC.
August 26, 2020



(Emery, Schlenker, Simon, and Taylor) regarding the nature and disposition of the soil and rock
exposed in the trench.

To document the observed exposures in the trench, a geologic log of the subsurface conditions
encountered was drawn. Detailed graphical representations of the subsurface conditions
encountered are presented on Figure 6, Log of Trench, and within the trench undisturbed thin-
wall tube and disturbed bulk samples of representative soils encountered were obtained for
subsequent laboratory testing and examination. The representative soil samples were placed in
sealed plastic bags and containers prior to transport to our laboratory.

Following the completion of excavation and logging, the test pits and trench were backfilled with
the excavated soils, but were not compacted in a controlled manner; thus, for any portion of the
proposed building placed over the trench areas, the backfill soils should be removed and
replaced with compacted structural fill.

3.4     LABORATORY TESTING

3.4.1   General

In order to provide data necessary for our engineering analyses, a laboratory testing program
was performed. The program included: Atterberg limits, partial gradation, direct shear, and
residual ring shear tests. The following paragraphs describe the tests and summarize the test
data.

3.4.2   Atterberg Limits Tests

To aid in classifying the site soils, an Atterberg limits test was performed upon three
representative samples of the soils encountered in the exploration trench. Results of the tests

4.     SITE CONDITIONS

4.1    SURFACE

The site is located in the Draper City Suncrest Development area. As shown on Figure 1,
Vicinity Map, the site is located at an elevation of approximately 6030 feet (msl). Figure 4,
LiDAR Analysis, indicates the property has surface elevations ranging from 6016 feet to over
6068 feet with slope gradients ranging from less than 5 percent to over 60 percent. The
topography of the site slopes downward toward the east and northeast. Vegetation on the site
is mostly a cover of native grasses and brush on the open areas with scrub oak and maple trees
covering the steeper slope areas on the west side of the property. The site is bordered on the
northwest by undeveloped land, on the south and west by residential properties, and on the east
by Oak Bluff Place (street).

4.2    SITE GEOLOGY

The geology of the site was interpreted through an integrated compilation of data analyses,
including a review of literature and mapping from our previous studies conducted in the area;
geologic mapping by (Biek, 2005) as shown on Figure 3, Geologic Mapping; photogeologic
analyses of 2012 imagery as shown on Figure 2, Site Plan, and Figure 5, Site Details; GIS
analyses of elevation and geoprocessed 2014 LiDAR terrain data as shown on Figure 4, LiDAR
Analysis, and available GIS hazards information provided by the Utah Automated Geographic
Reference Center (AGRC at: http://gis.utah.gov/); field reconnaissance of the general site area,
and the interpretation of the trench at the site as part of our field program. Seismic hazards
information was developed from United States Geologic Survey (USGS) databases (Peterson
and others, 2008).

The site is located on the Traverse Mountain Salient, which is within the Basin and Range
Physiographic Province. This province is characterized by approximately north-south trending
valleys and mountain ranges that have been formed by extensional tectonics and displacement
along normal faults. The province extends from the Wasatch Range on the east to the Sierra
Nevada Range on the west (Hunt, 1967). Conversely, the Traverse Mountain Salient is an east-
to-west structural ridge comprised largely of Tertiary volcanics and Paleozoic sedimentary rocks



                                                                                            Page 9
Breen Homes
                                                                                GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                        G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                            2
Geologic Hazard Evaluation – Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis
                                                                                ENGINEERING, INC.
August 26, 2020



(Biek, 2005), that is flanked by the Wasatch Range on the east and the Jordan Valley on the
west. The salient extends approximately 7.0 miles east to west and 3.5 miles north-to-south.

The near-surface geology of the subject site consists of igneous intrusives and volcaniclastic
sediments formed or deposited prior to the past 10,000 years (Holocene Epoch) (Beik, 2005).

4.3    SUBSURFACE SOIL AND GROUNDWATER

The soil and rock conditions encountered in the trench and shown on Figure 6, Log of Trench, to
the depths penetrated, consisted of surficial soil sequences, fills (Unit 4), colluvial deposits
(Unit 3), underlain by weathered volcanic ash layers (Units 2A and 2B), and indurated block and
ash tuff deposits (Unit 1).

The fill deposits (Unit 4) on the surface of the trench consisted of yellowish-brown Silty Clay
(CL), with Sand, Gravel and Cobbles, mantled with a weak surficial soil horizon. The fills were
observed to be one to three feet in thickness, with the fills partially covering the native soils in
the trench. Underlying the fills and extending the length of the trench, a well-developed Soil A-B
Horizon with vertical structures, one to two and one-half feet in thickness, covered the surface of
the underlying native deposits. The colluvial deposits (Unit 3) consisted of stiff, dark brown Silty
Clay (CL), with sub-angular Gravel and Cobbles. The colluvial deposits (Unit 3) were as much
as three feet in thickness and observed only on the west end of the trench, and partially overlaid
the weathered volcanic ash layers (Units 2A and 2B). The Units 2A and 2B weathered volcanic
ash layer sequence extended to the depth of the trench, was observed to be as much as eight
feet in thickness, and consisted of very stiff reddish-brown, (Unit 2A), and brown (Unit 2B) Silty
Clay (CH) with trace Sand and Gravel and few Cobbles; reddish-brown, (2A), and brown (2B),
fine to medium Sand, fine and coarse Gravel; light reddish-brown.

The Units 2A and 2B weathered volcanic ash layer beds were observed in the trench to dip
downward to the west 14- to 18-degrees, and to unconformably overlap the indurated volcanic
block and ash deposits (Unit 1). The Unit 1 indurated volcanic block and ash deposits were
observed as much as five feet in thickness and were exposed on the west end of the trench.
The Unit 1 indurated volcanic block and ash deposits consisted of very dense to indurated, light
yellowish-brown, Clay to Sand matrix weathered rock, with angular Gravel and Cobbles
(SM/GM), massive structure, with andesite and rhyolite blocks.

Along the Unit 1 and Unit 2A boundary, an approximately four-inch zone of deformed Unit 1
material, coated with a thin FeOX (iron oxide) vein was observed. This boundary was
interpreted during field review as a slope failure boundary, with block failure movement
occurring between Unit 1 and Units 2A and 2B. The 14- to 18-degree dips observed in Units 2A
and 2B are believed to be a result of back-tilting, as a result of this block failure movement.

The materials observed at depth in the trench; including Units 1, 2A, and 2B, are consistent with
the “block and ash-flow tuffs” geological description mapped by Biek (2005) for the Lehi

Breen Homes

                                                                              ENGINEERING, INC.
August 26, 2020



quadrangle and part of the Timpanogos Cave quadrangle as underlying the site. Biek’s (2005)
description is paraphrased as follows:

       Tv: Volcanic rocks of the east Traverse Mountains, undivided (Oligocene-Eocene) -
       Complexly interbedded block and ash-flow tuffs, volcanic mudflow breccia, minor lava
       flows, and minor fluvial volcaniclastic deposits in the east Traverse Mountains that are
       impractical to map separately due to poor exposures and extensive alteration; classified
       as borderline latite, trachyte, dacite, and andesite...block and ash-flow tuffs likely
       comprise the bulk of these deposits...(Beik, 2005).

Groundwater was not encountered in the trench or the 2016 test pits during excavation
operations; however, our experience is that ‘perched’ groundwater conditions may exist during
certain portions of the year. Seasonal and longer-term groundwater fluctuations on the order of
one to two feet are projected, with the highest seasonal levels generally occurring during the
late spring and early summer months. We do not anticipate that groundwater levels will affect
the proposed construction.

5.     ENGINEERING GEOLOGY AND SLOPE STABILITY ANALYSIS

5.1    STRONG GROUND MOTION

The site is located within Intermountain Seismic Belt and is near to the Salt Lake City section of
the Wasatch fault zone. Analyses of ground shaking hazard along the Wasatch Front suggests
that the Wasatch Fault Zone is the largest contributor to the seismic hazard in the Salt Lake City
area. The Wasatch fault zone is considered active and capable of generating earthquakes as
large as magnitude 7.3 (Arabasz and others,1992). Surface fault rupture commonly occurs in
conjunction with events of magnitude 6 or larger. DuRoss and Hylland (2015) have determined
that nine surface faulting earthquake events have occurred on the Salt Lake City Segment of
the Wasatch Fault Zone during the past 18,000 years.

Based on probabilistic estimates for the site (Peterson, and others, 2014), the expected peak
horizontal ground acceleration on rock from a large earthquake with a 10-percent probability of
exceedance in 50 years is as high as 0.21g, and for a 3.5-percent probability of exceedance in
50 years is as high as 0.50g for the site. Ground accelerations greater than these are possible
but will have a lower probability of occurrence

The 10-percent probability of exceedance in 50 years event has a return period of 475 years,
and the 0.21g acceleration for this event corresponds "very strong" perceived shaking with
"moderate" potential damage based on instrument intensity correlations. The 3.5-percent
probability of exceedance in 50 years event has a return period of 1400 years, and the 0.50g
acceleration for this event corresponds "very strong to severe" perceived shaking with
"moderate to heavy" potential damage based on instrument intensity correlations (Wald and
others,1999).


                                                                                           Page 11
Breen Homes
                                                                                GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                        G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                            2
Geologic Hazard Evaluation – Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis
                                                                                ENGINEERING, INC.
August 26, 2020



As per the Draper City Geologic Hazards Ordinance Section 12.1, ground motion parameters
for pseudo-static seismic stability evaluations shall be derived from the 3.5 percent probability of
exceedance in 50 years peak ground acceleration.

5.2    ACTIVE EARTHQUAKE FAULTS

Based upon our review of available literature, no active faults are known to pass through or
immediately adjacent to the site. The site is located outside of the fault investigation zones
identified by Draper City, on the 9-19-161-1; Appendix A, Geologic Hazards Study Area Maps,
Plate A-1, Surface Fault Rupture Hazard Special Study Area Map (Draper City, 2020). The
nearest Quaternary faulting to the property is the Salt Lake Segment of the Wasatch Fault Zone
(USGS ID Number 2351f), which is mapped as passing approximately 1.17 miles northeast of
the site (McDonald and others, 2018). The Wasatch Fault Zone is considered active because
Holocene (past 10,000 years) movement has been detected in studies conducted on this fault
zone (DuRoss and Hylland, 2015). The Wasatch Fault Zone is considered capable of
generating as large as magnitude 7.3 earthquakes (Arabasz, and others, 1992).

5.3    LIQUEFACTION

In conjunction with the ground shaking potential of large magnitude seismic events as discussed
previously, certain soil units may also possess a potential for liquefaction during a large
magnitude event. Liquefaction is a phenomenon whereby loose, saturated, granular soil units
lose a significant portion of their shear strength due to excess pore water pressure buildup
resulting from dynamic loading, such as that caused by an earthquake. Among other effects,
liquefaction can result in densification of such deposits, causing settlements of overlying layers
after an earthquake as excess pore water pressures are dissipated. Horizontally continuous
liquefied layers may also have a potential to spread laterally where sufficient slope or free-face
conditions exist. The primary factors affecting liquefaction potential of a soil deposit are:
(1) magnitude and duration of seismic ground motions; (2) soil type and consistency; and
(3) occurrence and depth to groundwater.

Liquefaction commonly occurs in saturated non-cohesive soils, such as alluvium, which
conditions are not found on the site. Consequently, the conditions susceptible to liquefaction do
not appear to be present at the site. Furthermore, the site is located outside of the liquefaction
study zones identified by Draper City, on the 9-19-161-1; Appendix A, Geologic Hazards Study
Area Maps, Plate A-2, Liquefaction Special Study Area Map (Draper City, 2020).

5.4    OTHER GEOLOGICAL HAZARDS

Other geological hazards considered for this study include debris flow hazards, stream scour
hazards, rock fall hazards, and shallow groundwater.

No alluvial fan deposits were identified in the vicinity of the site, additionally the site is located
outside of the debris flow study zones identified by Draper City, on the 9-19-161-1; Appendix A,
Geologic Hazards Study Area Maps, Plate A-4, Debris Flow Special Study Area Map (Draper
City, 2020), thus, we conclude debris flow hazards to be unlikely at the site. No alluvial
sediments were identified on the site; therefore, future stream scour hazards are also
considered unlikely at the site.

The site is not located down-slope from steep slope - bedrock outcrop areas where rockfall or
snow avalanche hazards may originate, and no such conditions were observed during our
mapping or reconnaissance of the site. The Draper City rockfall hazard map, on the
9-19-161-1; Appendix A, Geologic Hazards Study Area Maps, Plate A-5, Rockfall Special Study
Area Map, shows the site to be outside of this hazard area (Draper City, 2020),

Groundwater, nor incipient evidence thereof, was not observed in our trench or our 2016 test
pits; therefore, we do not expect shallow groundwater to be an issue at the site. However,
because of relatively shallow bedrock, we recommend that appropriate foundation drain
systems be considered for design.

A review of the FEMA Flood Insurance Rate Map for the site location (Map 49035C0625G),
indicates that the site is outside of the 100- and 500-year flood hazard areas delineated on the
FEMA maps (FEMA, 2009).

5.5    LANDSLIDING AND SLOPE STABILITY

A query of the LiDAR data indicates that slope gradients at the property site range from
moderate to steep with gradients ranging from less than 5 percent to in excess of 60 percent.
Draper City Code 9-16-040: Development Requirements specify that slope areas in excess of
thirty percent (30%) may not be developed… Our LiDAR analysis shown on Figure 4, LiDAR
Analysis, calculated the site slopes to be 31.4 percent for the 0.48-acre Lot 27 parcel, and
21.4 percent for the ground underlying the proposed structure location.

The surficial geological mapping by Biek (2005) describes the geological units underlying the
site to be of “complexly interbedded” Tertiary volcanic deposits of Eocene to Oligocene age
(roughly 50 to 29 million years before present). The soil and rock encountered in our trench
shown on Figure 6, and described in Section 3.3, was interpreted as a slope failure boundary,
with block failure movement occurring between Unit 1 and Units 2A and 2B. The planiform
geometry of the boundary is shown on Figure 5, as a geologic contact between units subdivided
herein as: Tv-b - Teritary volcanics - indurated block and ash deposits and Tv-a - Teritary
volcanics - weathered ash deposits. Because our review of the historic (pre-development
1958 and 1962, see Figures 8 and 9, respectively) aerial photography and LiDAR analysis
(2014) did not reveal diagnostic arcuate rotational morphology indicative of typical rotation
slumping (Varnes, 1978), and because the bedding of the volcanic weathered ash beds
(2A and 2B) on Figure 6, showed little evidence of deformation, we presume the slope failure

                                                                                ENGINEERING, INC.

boundary observed in the trench is the result of block failure movement, with the downslope
movement of the Tv-a - Teritary volcanics - weathered ash deposits, relative to the underlying
Tv-b - Teritary volcanics - indurated block and ash deposits. The strike of this movement is
believed to be elongated northwest to southeast and parallel with the geological contact shown on
Figure 5, also and parallel with the local controlling ridge and valley structure on the west side of
the Oak Bluff Place street. The sense of movement is down to the northeast. This topographic
structure is best visualized on the LiDAR imagery on Figure 4.

Figure 7, Geologic Cross-Section Line A-A’, shows the slope cross-section of the site area
marked as line A-A’ on Figures 5, Site Details. The cross-section traverses a 515-foot slope
area across the site location, beginning on the west on Saddle Leaf Court Street at
approximately 6090 feet and descending eastward across the site to the bottom of an unnamed
drainage east of the site to an elevation of 5976 feet. The Figure 7 cross-section illustrates
slope gradients and projected soil, rock, and groundwater conditions, and the possible failure
boundary to be used in our slope global slope stability calculations for the site.

5.5.1   Slope Stability Analysis

In order to evaluate the global stability of the sloping site, an analysis was performed with the
computer program, SLIDE (Version 6.0), utilizing the modified Bishops method for circular and
non-circular failure surfaces. The simulated annealing method was used for locating non-circular
surfaces. The analysis included both static and pseudo-seismic conditions.

The geometry and configuration for the global stability model was developed from the geologic
cross-section shown on Figure 5. Section A-A’ was determined to be the critical cross-section
for slope stability analysis based on the subsurface investigations, Lidar analysis, and proposed
site developments.

The subsurface profile was developed utilizing stratigraphic information obtained from the
geologic exploration trench as well as several test pits. The locations of the test pits, continuous
trench, and cross-section profile are shown on Figure 5.

As shown on model Run 1 and Run 2, the slope analysis limits were constrained on the cross-
section to below the location where the contact between Tv-a and Tv-b was observed. A dip
angle of this plane was measured at 30 degrees. A search for circular failure surfaces initiating
at the observed contact location and terminating at the unnamed drainage to the northeast of
the site was performed to find the critical slip surface. A minimum depth of 30 feet for potential
failure surfaces was specified in the model to reflect the observed initiation angle and geometry
of the potential shear plane.

The slope limits were also expanded to the entire cross-section to analyze the stability of the
slope above the Tv-a/Tv-b contact, as shown on model Run 3 and Run 4.

5.5.2   Soil Strength

Strength parameters were obtained through laboratory testing of disturbed and relatively
undisturbed samples as well as published correlations based on index soil parameters.
Subsurface investigations at the site identified evidence that the interface between weathered
ash deposits (fat clay) and indurated block and ash bedrock has possibly experienced
significant past shear deformation. Therefore, residual strength must be considered in the
analysis.

The Draper Geologic Hazards Ordinance states that residual shear strengths as low as 11 to
12 degrees have been measured in the Tertiary volcanics around Traverse Mountain. However,
if a friction angle of 11 degrees is utilized for the shear plane in this case, the slope would fail
under static conditions. A residual ring shear test was performed on a sample of the material
observed to be the basal shear surface of past deformation. The test resulted in a residual
friction angle of 19.5 degrees. It is difficult to know whether the material tested is the actual
shear plane since the deformed zone is often very thin. Correlations from Stark and Eid 1994,
and Stark and McCone, 2005, indicate a range of residual shear strengths of approximately
10 to 21 degrees. Considering the test results and available data, a middle-range residual
shear strength of 16 degrees was selected for residual strength acting along pre-sheared
surfaces within the slide mass. A shear strength of 19 degrees was selected for the remolded
strength acting along surfaces in a first-time shear scenario where fully residual conditions have
not yet developed.

Peak strengths are assumed for the underlying, un-deformed indurated block and ash bedrock.
Strength parameters were conservatively assumed for indurated block and ash bedrock to
bethat of a high-strength soil.

conditions. However, the factor of safety is less than the Draper City required minimum of 1.5.
Additionally, the slope is at risk of failure during the design seismic event.

5.5.5    Deformation Analysis

In order to estimate the potential movement of the unstable slide mass during a seismic event, a
deformation analysis was performed using the method proposed in Bray and Travasarou
(2007)1. The slide mass for this analysis is assumed to be the slip surface calculated from
Model Run 2 with constrained slope limits. The following parameters were utilized in the
deformation analysis:

         Yield Coefficient (ky) = 0.065 (back-calculated from pseudo-static slope stability model)

         Average Height of the Slide Mass (H) = 30 feet

         Average Shear Wave Velocity of the Slide Mass (Vs) = 2,000 fps (estimated)

         Initial Fundamental Period (Ts) = 4H/Vs = 0.06 seconds

         Moment Magnitude (Mw) = 7.0

         Spectral Acceleration at Degraded Period (Sa(1.5Ts)) = 0.78g

The calculated seismic displacement is 47cm with the above model inputs. To analyze the
sensitivity of the deformation analysis to the residual strength parameter, the analysis was run
utilizing a higher-end strength value of 19.5 degrees. With the higher strength parameter, the
yield coefficient ky = 0.135 and the calculated displacement is on the order of 17cm. This

1
         “Simplified Procedure for Estimating Earthquake-Induced Deviatoric Slope Displacements”
         Jonathan D. Bray and Thaleia Travasarou, Journal of Geotechnical and Geoenvironmental
         Engineering, ASCE, April 2007.

                                                                                              Page 18
Breen Homes
                                                                                GORDON
Job No. 224-023-19
                                                                        G       GEOTECHNICAL
                                                                            2
Geologic Hazard Evaluation – Geotechnical Slope Stability Analysis
                                                                                ENGINEERING, INC.
August 26, 2020



deformation is likely to be concentrated along the pre-existing shear plane. Model Runs 9
and 10 show derivation of the yield coefficients.

The results of the deformation analysis indicate that the slope will not meet the Draper City
required displacement threshold of 5cm. The deformation analysis calculation spreadsheet has
been included in Appendix C and supporting information in Appendix D.

5.5.6   Surficial Stability Analysis

Subsurface conditions at the site consist of indurated block and ash bedrock and weathered ash
deposits of which classify as fat clay soil. Due to the very low permeability of these materials, it
is our opinion that conditions do not exist that would allow for the development of an infinite
slope with groundwater seepage parallel to the slope.

6.      CONCLUSIONS

Based on the findings of this study, the slopes underlying the proposed structure location are
interpreted to have been subjected to past deformation as a result of block failure movement.
Detailed slope stability analyses indicate that the structures located below the Tv-a/Tv-b contact
will not meet the Draper Geologic Hazard Ordinance required minimum factor of safety against
slope failure.

The most preferable solution, from a geotechnical/geologic engineering standpoint, is hazard
avoidance. If other project constraints will allow, the proposed structure may be located off of
the unstable slide mass. The indurated block and ash bedrock on the hillside above the
Tv-a/Tv-b contact is stable and is suitable from a slope stability hazard standpoint. The
“Recommended Buildable Area” on Figure 5, shows the area on the property where the
Tv-a/Tv-b contact is within 15 feet from the surface of the site, and a conventional foundation
system is feasible.

If the structure cannot be located away from the unstable slide mass, the structure may
potentially be supported on a deep foundation system, such as soldier piles or drilled shafts,
installed through the unstable soil mass and into competent underlying materials and designed
to resist the potential shear forces. Soldier piles/drilled shafts can be used to stabilize the slope
and provide support for the structure (Blake and others, 2002); however, the depth to competent
materials in the lower portions of the lot could be 120 feet or more. Design of a deep foundation
system may require additional studies.

If the proposed dwelling is structurally placed upon the Tv-b - Teritary volcanics - indurated
block and ash deposits and penetrates the overlying Tv-a - Teritary volcanics - weathered ash
deposits, the site will be suitable for the proposed development from a geologic hazard
perspective.
